I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the element that appears most frequently in a list of strings
2. If there's a tie (no single element has a strictly higher frequency than others), we return None
3. We need to count frequencies and determine if one element has a majority (appears more than half the time)

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Analyze the examples
- `majority(["A", "B", "A"])` → "A" (A appears 2 times, B appears 1 time, A wins)
- `majority(["A", "B", "B", "A"])` → None (A and B both appear 2 times, it's a tie)

### Step 2: Algorithm approach
1. Count the frequency of each element in the list
2. Find the maximum frequency
3. Check if any element has frequency > n/2 (strict majority)
4. If yes, return that element; otherwise return None

### Step 3: Implementation details
- Use a dictionary or Counter to count frequencies
- Find the maximum frequency
- Check if the maximum frequency is greater than n/2
- If it is, we have a majority; if not, no majority exists

### Step 4: Edge cases
- Empty list → should return None
- All elements same → should return that element
- All elements different → should return None

```python
def majority(arr):
    if not arr:
        return None
    
    # Count frequency of each element
    freq = {}
    for element in arr:
        freq[element] = freq.get(element, 0) + 1
    
    # Find the maximum frequency
    max_freq = max(freq.values())
    n = len(arr)
    
    # Check if any element appears more than half the time
    # For majority, we need frequency > n/2
    majority_threshold = n / 2
    
    # Find if there's an element with frequency strictly greater than n/2
    for element, count in freq.items():
        if count > majority_threshold:
            return element
    
    return None
```

I'll solve this step-by-step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the element that appears most frequently in a list of strings
2. If there's a clear majority (appears more than half the time), return that element
3. If no single element appears more than half the time, return None

## Step-by-Step Analysis

Let me trace through the examples:
- `["A", "B", "A"]`: A appears 2 times, B appears 1 time. A appears more than half (2/3), so return "A"
- `["A", "B", "B", "A"]`: A appears 2 times, B appears 2 times. Neither appears more than half (4/2 = 2), so return None

## Key Insight
For a majority element, it must appear more than n/2 times where n is the total length of the array. If no element appears more than n/2 times, there's no majority.

## Algorithm Steps
1. Count the frequency of each element in the list
2. Find the element with the highest frequency
3. Check if that frequency is greater than len(arr) / 2
4. If yes, return that element; otherwise return None

## Implementation

```python
def majority(arr):
    if not arr:
        return None
    
    # Count frequency of each element
    freq = {}
    for element in arr:
        freq[element] = freq.get(element, 0) + 1
    
    # Find the element with maximum frequency
    max_count = 0
    majority_element = None
    
    for element, count in freq.items():
        if count > max_count:
            max_count = count
            majority_element = element
    
    # Check if the majority element appears more than half the time
    if max_count > len(arr) / 2:
        return majority_element
    else:
        return None
```

The solution works as follows:
- Handles empty array edge case
- Uses a dictionary to count frequencies
- Identifies the element with maximum frequency
- Checks if that frequency exceeds half the total length (majority condition)
- Returns the element if it's a majority, otherwise returns None

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step-by-step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the element that appears most frequently in a list of strings
2. If there's a tie (no single element appears more than any other), return None
3. We need to count frequencies and determine if there's a clear majority

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Analyze the examples
- `majority(["A", "B", "A"])` → "A" (A appears 2 times, B appears 1 time, so A is majority)
- `majority(["A", "B", "B", "A"])` → None (A and B both appear 2 times, so no clear majority)

### Step 2: Algorithm design
1. Count the frequency of each element in the list
2. Find the maximum frequency
3. Check if any element occurs exactly once at that maximum frequency
4. If exactly one element has the maximum frequency, return that element
5. Otherwise, return None

### Step 3: Implementation considerations
- Use a dictionary or Counter to count frequencies
- Find the maximum frequency
- Count how many elements have that maximum frequency
- If only one element has the maximum frequency, return it; otherwise return None

### Step 4: Code implementation

```python
def majority(arr):
    if not arr:
        return None
    
    # Count frequency of each element
    freq = {}
    for element in arr:
        freq[element] = freq.get(element, 0) + 1
    
    # Find the maximum frequency
    max_freq = max(freq.values())
    
    # Count how many elements have the maximum frequency
    max_count = sum(1 for count in freq.values() if count == max_freq)
    
    # If exactly one element has the maximum frequency, return it
    if max_count == 1:
        # Find which element has the maximum frequency
        for element, count in freq.items():
            if count == max_freq:
                return element
    
    # If multiple elements tie for maximum frequency, return None
    return None
```

The solution works as follows:
1. Handle empty list case (returns None)
2. Count frequencies using a dictionary
3. Find the maximum frequency value
4. Count how many elements have this maximum frequency
5. If exactly one element has the maximum frequency, return that element
6. Otherwise (tie), return None

This handles both the given examples correctly and follows the requirement for the majority element to be unique.

        for element, count in freq.items():

    return None

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step-by-step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the element that appears most frequently in a list of strings
2. If multiple elements tie for the highest frequency (i.e., there's no unique majority), we should return None
3. We need to count frequencies and determine if there's exactly one element with the maximum frequency

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Analyze the examples
- `majority(["A", "B", "A"])` → "A" (A appears 2 times, B appears 1 time, so A is the majority)
- `majority(["A", "B", "B", "A"])` → None (A and B both appear 2 times, so no unique majority)

### Step 2: Algorithm design
1. Count the frequency of each element in the list
2. Find the maximum frequency
3. Check how many elements have this maximum frequency
4. If exactly one element has the maximum frequency, return that element
5. Otherwise, return None

### Step 3: Implementation approach
- Use a dictionary to count frequencies
- Find the maximum frequency value
- Iterate through the frequencies to count how many elements achieve this maximum
- If count is 1, return the element with maximum frequency; otherwise return None

### Step 4: Edge cases to consider
- Empty list
- All elements identical (should return that element)
- All elements different (should return None)

## Code Implementation

```python
def majority(arr):
    if not arr:
        return None
    
    # Count frequency of each element
    frequency = {}
    for element in arr:
        frequency[element] = frequency.get(element, 0) + 1
    
    # Find the maximum frequency
    max_frequency = max(frequency.values())
    
    # Count how many elements have the maximum frequency
    max_count = 0
    winner = None
    
    for element, count in frequency.items():
        if count == max_frequency:
            max_count += 1
            if max_count == 1:
                winner = element
    
    # If exactly one element has the maximum frequency, return it
    return winner
```

The solution works as follows:
1. Handle empty array case by returning None
2. Count frequency of each element using a dictionary
3. Find the maximum frequency
4. Iterate through the frequency dictionary to find all elements with maximum frequency
5. If exactly one such element exists (max_count == 1), return it; otherwise return None

This correctly handles both examples:
- ["A", "B", "A"]: A has frequency 2, B has frequency 1 → only A has max frequency → returns "A"
- ["A", "B", "B", "A"]: A and B both have frequency 2 → two elements have max frequency → returns None

                winner = element

    return winner

I'll solve this step-by-step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the element that appears most frequently in a list of strings
2. If multiple elements have the same highest frequency, we return None
3. We need to count frequencies and determine if there's a unique majority

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Analyze the examples
- `majority(["A", "B", "A"])` → "A" (A appears 2 times, B appears 1 time, so A is majority)
- `majority(["A", "B", "B", "A"])` → None (A and B both appear 2 times, so no unique majority)

### Step 2: Algorithm design
1. Count frequency of each element using a dictionary or Counter
2. Find the maximum frequency
3. Check if exactly one element has this maximum frequency
4. If yes, return that element; otherwise return None

### Step 3: Implementation approach
- Use a dictionary to count occurrences
- Find the maximum count
- Identify which elements have this maximum count
- Return the element if only one has the maximum count, otherwise None

### Step 4: Edge cases to consider
- Empty list? The problem doesn't specify, but we should handle it
- Single element list? That would have a clear majority
- All elements the same? That would have a clear majority
- All elements different? No majority, return None

## Code Implementation

```python
def majority(arr):
    if not arr:
        return None
    
    # Count frequency of each element
    freq = {}
    for element in arr:
        freq[element] = freq.get(element, 0) + 1
    
    # Find the maximum frequency
    max_freq = max(freq.values())
    
    # Find how many elements have the maximum frequency
    max_elements = [element for element, count in freq.items() if count == max_freq]
    
    # If only one element has the maximum frequency, return it
    if len(max_elements) == 1:
        return max_elements[0]
    else:
        return None
```

The solution works as follows:
1. Handle empty list edge case
2. Count frequencies using a dictionary
3. Find the maximum frequency value
4. Find all elements that have this maximum frequency
5. If exactly one element has the maximum frequency, return it; otherwise return None

This correctly handles both examples:
- `["A", "B", "A"]`: A appears 2 times, B appears 1 time → only A has max frequency → return "A"
- `["A", "B", "B", "A"]`: A and B both appear 2 times → two elements have max frequency → return None

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step-by-step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the element that appears most frequently in a list of strings
2. If there's no clear majority (i.e., no element appears more than half the time), return None
3. A majority means an element appears more than n/2 times in a list of n elements

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Analyze the examples
- `majority(["A", "B", "A"])`: A appears 2 times, B appears 1 time. Total elements = 3, so majority threshold = 3/2 = 1.5. Since A appears 2 times > 1.5, return "A"
- `majority(["A", "B", "B", "A"])`: A appears 2 times, B appears 2 times. Total elements = 4, so majority threshold = 4/2 = 2. No element appears > 2 times, so return None

### Step 2: Algorithm Design
1. Count the frequency of each element in the list
2. Find the element with the highest frequency
3. Check if that frequency is strictly greater than n/2
4. If yes, return that element; otherwise return None

### Step 3: Implementation Strategy
- Use a dictionary to count frequencies
- Iterate through the list to build the frequency map
- Find the maximum frequency and which element has it
- Compare maximum frequency with (n/2) + 1 (since we need strictly more than half)

### Step 4: Edge Cases
- Empty list: should return None
- All elements same: should return that element
- Equal frequencies: no majority, return None

```python
def majority(arr):
    if not arr:
        return None
    
    # Count frequency of each element
    freq = {}
    for element in arr:
        freq[element] = freq.get(element, 0) + 1
    
    # Find the maximum frequency and the corresponding element
    max_freq = 0
    majority_element = None
    
    for element, count in freq.items():
        if count > max_freq:
            max_freq = count
            majority_element = element
    
    # Check if this is a true majority (appears more than n/2 times)
    n = len(arr)
    majority_threshold = n / 2
    
    if max_freq > majority_threshold:
        return majority_element
    else:
        return None
```

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the element that appears most frequently in a list of strings
2. If there's a clear majority (one element appears more than half the time), return that element
3. If no single element appears more than half the time, return None

## Step-by-Step Analysis

1. **What is a majority?** In this context, a majority means an element that appears more than half the total count. For a list of length n, an element has a majority if it appears more than n/2 times.

2. **Example breakdown:**
   - ["A", "B", "A"]: A appears 2 times, total is 3, 2 > 3/2 = 1.5 → A has majority
   - ["A", "B", "B", "A"]: A appears 2 times, B appears 2 times, total is 4, 2 = 4/2 = 2 → no element appears more than half → return None

3. **Algorithm steps:**
   - Count frequency of each element
   - Find the maximum frequency
   - Check if maximum frequency > n/2
   - If yes, return the element with maximum frequency
   - If no, return None

## Implementation

```python
def majority(arr):
    if not arr:
        return None
    
    # Count frequency of each element
    frequency = {}
    for element in arr:
        frequency[element] = frequency.get(element, 0) + 1
    
    # Find the maximum frequency
    max_count = 0
    majority_element = None
    
    for element, count in frequency.items():
        if count > max_count:
            max_count = count
            majority_element = element
    
    # Check if this is a true majority (more than half)
    if max_count > len(arr) / 2:
        return majority_element
    else:
        return None
```

## Verification with examples

1. majority(["A", "B", "A"]):
   - Frequency: A:2, B:1
   - Max count: 2
   - 2 > 3/2 = 1.5 → True
   - Returns "A" ✓

2. majority(["A", "B", "B", "A"]):
   - Frequency: A:2, B:2
   - Max count: 2
   - 2 > 4/2 = 2 → False (2 is not greater than 2)
   - Returns None ✓

The solution correctly handles the majority condition - the element must appear strictly more than half the time.

Submitted Code

View Extracted Code 
def majority(arr):
    if not arr:
        return None
    
    # Count frequency of each element
    frequency = {}
    for element in arr:
        frequency[element] = frequency.get(element, 0) + 1
    
    # Find the maximum frequency
    max_count = 0
    majority_element = None
    
    for element, count in frequency.items():
        if count > max_count:
            max_count = count
            majority_element = element
    
    # Check if this is a true majority (more than half)
    if max_count > len(arr) / 2:
        return majority_element
    else:
        return None

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step-by-step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the element that appears most frequently in a list of strings
2. If there's a clear majority (one element appears more than half the time), return that element
3. If no single element appears more than half the time, return None

## Step-by-Step Analysis

1. **What is a majority?**
   - A majority element appears more than n/2 times in a list of n elements
   - For example, in ["A", "B", "A"] (n=3), A appears 2 times, which is > 3/2 = 1.5, so A is majority
   - In ["A", "B", "B", "A"] (n=4), A appears 2 times, B appears 2 times, neither appears more than 4/2 = 2 times, so no majority

2. **Algorithm approach:**
   - Count frequency of each element
   - Find which element has frequency > n/2
   - Return that element if found, otherwise return None

3. **Implementation steps:**
   - Use a dictionary or Counter to count frequencies
   - Iterate through the array to count occurrences
   - Check if any element's count > len(arr)/2
   - Return the majority element or None

## Edge cases to consider:
- Empty list: should return None
- All elements the same: should return that element
- Equal frequencies: no majority, return None

```python
def majority(arr):
    if not arr:
        return None
    
    # Count frequency of each element
    freq = {}
    for element in arr:
        freq[element] = freq.get(element, 0) + 1
    
    # Find if any element appears more than half the time
    n = len(arr)
    threshold = n / 2
    
    for element, count in freq.items():
        if count > threshold:
            return element
    
    return None
```

def majority(arr):
    if not arr:
        return None
    
    # Count frequency of each element
    freq = {}
    for element in arr:
        freq[element] = freq.get(element, 0) + 1
    
    # Find if any element appears more than half the time
    n = len(arr)
    threshold = n / 2
    
    for element, count in freq.items():
        if count > threshold:
            return element
    
    return None

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×n matrix initially filled with zeros
2. We need to place exactly k ones in the matrix
3. The matrix must be symmetric with respect to the main diagonal (if cell (i,j) has a 1, then cell (j,i) must also have a 1)
4. The resulting matrix should be lexicographically maximal
5. If no such matrix exists, return -1

## Key Insights

1. **Symmetry constraint**: For a symmetric matrix, if we place a 1 at position (i,j) where i ≠ j, we must also place a 1 at position (j,i). This means each off-diagonal pair consumes 2 ones.

2. **Diagonal positions**: For positions where i = j (main diagonal), placing a 1 only consumes 1 one.

3. **Lexicographically maximal**: To make the matrix lexicographically maximal, we should try to place ones as early as possible in the row order (top to bottom) and within each row, from left to right.

4. **Feasibility**: We need to check if k ones can be placed given the symmetry constraint.

## Feasibility Analysis

- Let's say we place `o` pairs of ones off-diagonal (each pair consumes 2 ones)
- Total ones = d + 2o = k
- So k must be achievable as d + 2o where d ≥ 0 and o ≥ 0
- This means k must be at least 0, and if k > 0, we need k ≥ d (with d ≤ n)

## Construction Strategy

To make the matrix lexicographically maximal:

1. We want to place ones as early as possible (row by row, then column by column)
2. For diagonal positions, we place ones when possible
3. For off-diagonal positions, we place symmetric pairs

Actually, since we want lexicographically maximal, we need to prioritize placing ones in the earlier positions.

For a symmetric matrix:
- Position (0,0) is first - we can place a 1 here (costs 1)
- Then (0,1) and (1,0) - if we place a 1 at (0,1), we also place at (1,0) (costs 2)
- Then (0,2) and (2,0) - costs 2
- And so on.

   a. For each column j from 0 to n-1:
      i. If i == j (diagonal), we can place a 1 here
      ii. If i < j (off-diagonal), we can place a 1 at (i,j) and (j,i)

- etc.

But wait, (0,1) and (1,0) are symmetric, so placing at (0,1) also places at (1,0).

So the optimal strategy is:
1. For each row i from 0 to n-1:
   a. For each column j from 0 to n-1:
      i. If i == j (diagonal), we can place a 1
      ii. If i < j (upper triangle), we can place a 1 at (i,j) which will also place at (j,i)

But when we place a 1 at (i,j) where i < j, we're placing two ones (at (i,j) and (j,i)).

So the algorithm:
1. If k > n*(n-1) + 1, return -1
2. Initialize an n×n matrix of zeros
3. For each i from 0 to n-1:
   a. For each j from 0 to n-1:
      i. If i == j: position (i,i) - costs 1 one
      ii. If i < j: position (i,j) and (j,i) - costs 2 ones
   b. But we need to do it in a way that maximizes lexicographic order

Actually, since we go row by row and within row column by column, and we want to place ones at the earliest positions possible, we should:

- Start with the upper triangle (including diagonal) in order of increasing i,j

For each (i,j) with i ≤ j:
- If we can place a 1 at (i,j) without exceeding k, place it
- For diagonal (i=i): this costs 1 one
- For off-diagonal (i<j): this costs 2 ones

But we need to be careful: when we place at (i,j) with i<j, it also places at (j,i), which is fine.

So the algorithm:
1. Check if k > n*(n-1) + 1 → return -1
2. Create matrix of zeros
3. Set current_ones = 0
4. For i in range(n):
   For j in range(i, n):  # i ≤ j
      if i == j:  # diagonal
          if current_ones + 1 <= k:
              matrix[i][j] = 1
              current_ones += 1
      else:  # i < j, off-diagonal
          if current_ones + 2 <= k:
              matrix[i][j] = 1
              matrix[j][i] = 1
              current_ones += 2
5. Output the matrix

Could we do better? What if we skip (0,0) and place (0,1) and (1,0) first?
- That would give [[0,1,0],[1,0,0],[0,0,0]] - but first row starts with 0, which is worse than [1,1,0].

So our approach is correct.

Wait, actually in our algorithm, we place (0,0) first because it comes before (0,1) in the iteration order.

For lexicographic maximality, we want the first row to be as large as possible in the first position, so we should prioritize diagonal positions.

Our order (i,j with i ≤ j) and going row by row, column by column, ensures we place the earliest possible positions.

Therefore, the solution is:

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())

# Check feasibility
max_ones = n * (n - 1) + 1
if k > max_ones:
    print(-1)
else:
    # Create matrix
    matrix = [[0] * n for _ in range(n)]
    ones_used = 0
    
    # Go through upper triangle (including diagonal)
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i, n):
            if i == j:  # diagonal element
                if ones_used + 1 <= k:
                    matrix[i][j] = 1
                    ones_used += 1
            else:  # off-diagonal element (i < j)
                if ones_used + 2 <= k:
                    matrix[i][j] = 1
                    matrix[j][i] = 1
                    ones_used += 2
    
    # Output the matrix
    for row in matrix:
        print(' '.join(map(str, row)))
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×n matrix initially filled with zeros
2. We need to place exactly k ones in the matrix
3. The matrix must be symmetric with respect to the main diagonal (if matrix[i][j] = 1, then matrix[j][i] = 1)
4. The result should be lexicographically maximal
5. If impossible, output -1

## Key Observations

1. **Symmetry constraint**: For every position (i,j) where we place a 1, we must also place a 1 at (j,i). This means:
   - For i ≠ j: each pair (i,j) and (j,i) counts as 2 ones
   - For i = j (main diagonal): each diagonal position counts as 1 one

2. **Lexicographically maximal**: To maximize lexicographically, we want to place ones as early as possible in each row (from left to right).

3. **Feasibility condition**: 
   - If we place a 1 on the diagonal, it contributes 1 to the total count
   - If we place 1s off-diagonal, they contribute 2 to the total count
   - So we need k to be achievable with combinations of diagonal and off-diagonal placements

4. **Maximum possible ones**: 
   - Maximum diagonal ones: n (one in each diagonal position)
   - Maximum off-diagonal pairs: C(n,2) = n*(n-1)/2 pairs, each giving 2 ones
   - Total maximum ones = n + 2*(n*(n-1)/2) = n + n*(n-1) = n²

## Feasibility Analysis

Let's say we place:
- d ones on the diagonal (0 ≤ d ≤ n)
- p pairs of off-diagonal ones (0 ≤ p ≤ n*(n-1)/2)

Total ones = d + 2p = k

So k must satisfy: k ≥ 0 and k ≤ n², and k must have the same parity as d (since d + 2p has the same parity as d).

Since d can be chosen to have any parity (0 to n), we can achieve any k that is between 0 and n², except when k is greater than n².

But more precisely: if k > n², it's impossible.

If k ≤ n², we need to check if k can be written as d + 2p where 0 ≤ d ≤ n and 0 ≤ p ≤ n*(n-1)/2.

Since d can be chosen from 0 to n, we can achieve any k such that k ≤ n² and k has the same parity as some d in [0,n]. Since we can choose d in {0,1,2,...,n}, we can achieve any k ≤ n² as long as k ≤ n² and k has the same parity as some number in 0 to n.

Actually, since we can choose d to be k mod 2 (if k mod 2 ≤ n), and since k mod 2 can be 0 or 1, and both 0 and 1 are ≤ n for n ≥ 1, we can achieve any k such that 0 ≤ k ≤ n² and k ≤ n².

Wait, let me reconsider: if k > n², impossible.
If k ≤ n², is it always possible?

For k ≤ n², we need to find d in [0,n] and p in [0, n*(n-1)/2] such that d + 2p = k.

We can choose d = k mod 2, but we also need d ≤ n and d ≥ 0.

Since k ≥ 0 and d = k mod 2 is either 0 or 1, and both 0 and 1 are ≤ n for any n ≥ 1, we can always choose d = k mod 2 (if k mod 2 ≤ n, which it is), then p = (k - d) / 2.

We need to check if p ≤ n*(n-1)/2.

If (k - d) / 2 ≤ n*(n-1)/2, which simplifies to k - d ≤ n*(n-1).

Since d ≥ 0, k ≤ n*(n-1) + d ≤ n*(n-1) + 1.

But since k ≤ n² = n*(n-1) + n, we need k - d ≤ n*(n-1).

This means k ≤ n*(n-1) + d ≤ n*(n-1) + 1.

But k can be as large as n² = n*(n-1) + n, and for k > n*(n-1) + 1, we'd need d > 1, which isn't possible.

I think I made an error. Let me reconsider.

We have d + 2p = k, with 0 ≤ d ≤ n, 0 ≤ p ≤ n*(n-1)/2.

So 0 ≤ k ≤ n + 2*(n*(n-1)/2) = n + n*(n-1) = n².

For any k in [0, n²], we want to find valid d and p.

We can choose p such that p = floor((k - d)/2) and select d to make this work.

We need:
1. d ≤ n
2. p ≥ 0 → d ≤ k
3. p ≤ n*(n-1)/2 → (k - d)/2 ≤ n*(n-1)/2 → k - d ≤ n*(n-1) → d ≥ k - n*(n-1)

Since d ≥ 0, we need max(0, k - n*(n-1)) ≤ d ≤ min(n, k)

For this interval to be non-empty, we need max(0, k - n*(n-1)) ≤ min(n, k)

This is always true if k ≤ n².

- If k ≤ n*(n-1): max(0, k - n*(n-1)) = 0, min(n, k) = k, and k ≥ 0, so 0 ≤ k, so d can be chosen as 0
- If k > n*(n-1): max(0, k - n*(n-1)) = k - n*(n-1), min(n, k) = n, so we need k - n*(n-1) ≤ n → k ≤ n*(n-1) + n = n²

And since k ≤ n², this holds.

So for any k ∈ [0, n²], we can find valid d and p.

Therefore, the condition is simply: if k > n², output -1. Otherwise, construct the matrix.

## Lexicographically Maximal Construction

To be lexicographically maximal, we want to place ones as early as possible in each row.

Strategy:
1. First, place the maximum possible ones on the diagonal (to maximize early positions)
2. Then place off-diagonal ones in the top-left positions (to maximize lexicographically)

But actually, we need to be more strategic.

Since we want lexicographic maximality, we should process rows from top to bottom, and within each row from left to right.

For each position (i,j), if we place a 1 there, we must also place a 1 at (j,i). 

The only positions that don't have a symmetric counterpart are the diagonal.

To maximize lexicographically:
- We should prefer diagonal positions over off-diagonal ones, because placing a 1 on diagonal affects only position (i,i), while off-diagonal ones affect two positions (i,j) and (j,i).
- But lexicographically, we want to set (0,0) to 1 if possible, then (0,1) to 1 if possible, etc.

Actually, the diagonal positions are critical because they appear in position (i,i) which is early in each row.

But let's think about it: if we place a 1 at (i,j) for i < j, it contributes to row i at column j, and row j at column i. Since we want lexicographic maximality, we need to ensure that in each row, we have ones as early as possible.

The key insight is that we should prioritize positions in the upper triangle (i < j) because they affect both the i-th row and j-th row.

But since the matrix is symmetric, placing a 1 at (i,j) with i < j affects row i at column j and row j at column i.

To maximize lexicographically, we should try to place ones in the upper triangle, but we must ensure that in each row, the ones come in increasing column order.

Actually, the optimal strategy is:
1. First, place as many diagonal ones as possible (since they give us a 1 at position (i,i) which is the first position in row i)
2. Then fill the upper triangle (i < j) with ones to satisfy the remaining count

But we can do better: we should process positions in a specific order.

Optimal greedy approach for lexicographic maximality:
- Process positions in row-major order: (0,0), (0,1), (0,2), ..., (1,0), (1,1), ...
- For each position, if placing a 1 there doesn't violate symmetry or exceed k, and if it helps achieve lexicographic maximality, place it.

But we need to be careful: placing a 1 at (i,j) with i < j would require placing at (j,i), consuming 2 ones.

Placing at (i,i) consumes 1 one.

Since (i,i) affects only position (i,i) in row i, and (i,j) with i < j affects both (i,j) and (j,i), we should place diagonal ones first because they give us an immediate 1 in each row at the diagonal position, which is a prime requirement for lexicographic maximality.

Actually, let's think about it: in row 0, we want position (0,0) to be 1 if possible. This can only be achieved by placing a diagonal one at (0,0). Similarly for other rows.

So to maximize lexicographically, we should:
1. Place 1s on the diagonal as much as possible
2. For off-diagonal, place 1s in the upper triangle (i < j)

- First place 1s on the diagonal for positions (i,i) where i is as small as possible (to maximize row 0, then row 1, etc.)
- Then place off-diagonal 1s in positions (i,j) where i < j, starting from the smallest i and j

But the question is: how many diagonal ones to place?

We want to maximize lexicographic order, so we should place diagonal ones at the beginning of each row.

For lexicographic maximality, we want to place ones in the earliest possible positions.

In the row-major order, position (i,j) appears before position (i,j+1), so we need to fill from left to right.

The optimal strategy is to use all the off-diagonal pairs in the upper triangle, starting from (0,1), (0,2), ... and so on, and place diagonal ones where needed.

- In row 0: we want 1 at (0,0) if possible, then at (0,1), then (0,2), etc.

2. Then place off-diagonal ones in the upper triangle (i < j) in row-major order.

But actually, we might be able to place off-diagonal ones earlier in the row, but since they appear at column j > i, and we're already placing diagonal ones, the diagonal one at (i,i) comes first.

The key is that we have to satisfy d + 2p = k.

To maximize lexicographic order, we should minimize the "delay" of ones in early columns.

The best is to have 1s in the diagonal positions as much as possible, and then in the off-diagonal positions for i < j.

But we have flexibility in choosing d and p.

Since we want lexicographic maximality, and diagonal positions come earlier in the row-major order, we should prioritize diagonal placements.

The algorithm:
1. If k > n², output -1
2. Find d and p such that d + 2p = k, with 0 ≤ d ≤ n, 0 ≤ p ≤ n*(n-1)//2

For lexicographic maximality, we want the first different entry between two matrices to be as large as possible.

But due to symmetry, if we place a 1 at (i,j), we must also place at (j,i).

For i < j, placing at (i,j) affects positions (i,j) and (j,i).
For i = j, placing at (i,i) affects only (i,i).

In terms of lexicographic order, the positions are ordered by row, then by column.

So the order is:
- (0,0) - row 0, col 0
- (0,1) - row 0, col 1  
- (0,2) - row 0, col 2
- (1,0) - row 1, col 0
- (1,1) - row 1, col 1
- (1,2) - row 1, col 2
- etc.

The key insight: position (i,j) comes before (i,j+1) and before (i+1,0), etc.

Specifically, for any i, j, (i,j) comes before (i+1,0).

Therefore, to maximize lexicographically, we should place 1s in the order of increasing row, then increasing column.

The constraint is that if we place a 1 at (i,j) with i < j, we also need to place at (j,i).

The positions (0,0), (0,1), (0,2), ..., (n-1,0), (n-1,1), ... all form a sequence.

But due to symmetry, if we place at (i,j) with i < j, we're also placing at (j,i).

In the lexicographic ordering, (i,j) with i < j appears after (j,i) only if j > i, but j > i means column j > column i.

For example, (0,1) appears before (1,0)? No! In row-major order, (0,1) comes before (1,0) because row 0 < row 1.

So (0,1) comes before (1,0) in row-major order.

Therefore, the positions in lexicographic order are:
(0,0), (0,1), (0,2), ..., (0,n-1), (1,0), (1,1), (1,2), ..., (1,n-1), ..., (n-1,0), (n-1,1), ..., (n-1,n-1)

The lexicographic order is by row first, then by column.

So (0,0) is first, then (0,1), then (0,2), ..., then (0,n-1), then (1,0), then (1,1), etc.

For symmetry, we need to place ones at symmetric positions.

The optimal strategy is to place ones in this order, and for each position (i,j) we consider:
- If i == j: placing a 1 consumes 1 from k, and sets A[i][j] = 1
- If i < j: placing a 1 consumes 2 from k (because we also need to set A[j][i] = 1), and sets A[i][j] = 1 and A[j][i] = 1
- If i > j: this would be symmetric to placing at (j,i), so we skip it

So the algorithm:
1. If k > n², output -1
2. Otherwise, create an n×n matrix of zeros
3. For each position (i,j) in row-major order:
   - If i == j: if k >= 1, place a 1 at (i,i) and reduce k by 1
   - If i < j: if k >= 2, place 1s at (i,j) and (j,i), and reduce k by 2
4. After processing, if k > 0, we have a problem (should not happen since k <= n²)
5. Output the matrix

Wait, is this correct? Let's check with examples.

Example 1: n=2, k=1
- Row 0: (0,0): i==j, k=1 >= 1, so place 1 at (0,0), k becomes 0
- Row 0: (0,1): i=0 < j=1, k=0 < 2, skip
- Row 1: (1,0): already covered by symmetry of (0,1)
- Row 1: (1,1): i==j, k=0 < 1, skip
Result: [[1,0],[0,0]] ✓

Example 2: n=3, k=2
- (0,0): i==j, k=2 >= 1, place 1 at (0,0), k=1
- (0,1): i=0 < j=1, k=1 < 2, skip
- (0,2): i=0 < j=2, k=1 < 2, skip
- (1,0): already covered by symmetry of (0,1), skip
- (1,1): i==j, k=1 >= 1, place 1 at (1,1), k=0
- (1,2): k=0 < 2, skip
- (2,0): skip
- (2,1): skip
- (2,2): k=0 < 1, skip
Result: [[1,0,0],[0,1,0],[0,0,0]] ✓

Example 3: n=2, k=5
- n² = 4, k=5 > 4, so output -1 ✓

## Implementation

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())

# Check if k is possible
max_ones = n * n
if k > max_ones:
    print(-1)
else:
    # Create n x n matrix filled with zeros
    matrix = [[0] * n for _ in range(n)]
    
    # Process positions in row-major order
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(n):
            if i == j:  # Diagonal
                if k >= 1:
                    matrix[i][j] = 1
                    k -= 1
            elif i < j:  # Upper triangle (i < j)
                if k >= 2:
                    matrix[i][j] = 1
                    matrix[j][i] = 1
                    k -= 2
    
    # After processing, k should be 0
    # If k > 0, it means we couldn't place all ones, but our feasibility check above ensures this won't happen
    # However, if k > 0, we should output -1? No, our feasibility check should ensure k <= n² and we can always place exactly k ones
    # Actually, our greedy placement will always use exactly k ones because we check the conditions properly
    
    # Output the matrix
    for row in matrix:
        print(' '.join(map(str, row)))
```

One matrix is lexicographically greater than the other if the first different number in the first different row from the top in the first matrix is greater than the corresponding number in the second one.

```python
else:
    # Create n x n matrix filled with zeros
    matrix = [[0] * n for _ in range(n)]
    
    # Fill the matrix in lexicographically maximal way
    # Process positions where i <= j (upper triangular including diagonal)
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(n):
            if i > j:
                continue  # Skip positions below diagonal (will be handled by symmetry)
            
            if i == j:  # Diagonal element
                if k >= 1:
                    matrix[i][j] = 1
                    k -= 1
            elif i < j:  # Off-diagonal element (i < j)
                if k >= 2:
                    matrix[i][j] = 1
                    matrix[j][i] = 1
                    k -= 2
    
    # Output the matrix
    for row in matrix:
        print(' '.join(map(str, row)))
```

4. The resulting matrix should be lexicographically maximal

## Key Insights

1. **Symmetry constraint**: For every off-diagonal position (i,j) where i ≠ j, if we place a 1 at (i,j), we must also place a 1 at (j,i). This means each such pair consumes 2 ones.

2. **Diagonal constraint**: For positions on the diagonal (i,i), placing a 1 only consumes 1 one.

3. **Lexicographically maximal**: To maximize lexicographically, we want to place ones as early as possible in row-major order. This means we should prioritize placing ones in earlier rows and columns.

4. **Feasibility check**: 
   - If k is odd and n > 1, we can only have an odd number of ones if we use an odd number of diagonal positions (since off-diagonal pairs consume even numbers of ones)
   - If k is too large, we can't place k ones with the symmetry constraint

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())

# Check if k is too large
if k > n * n:
    print(-1)
else:
    # Create matrix filled with zeros
    matrix = [[0] * n for _ in range(n)]
    remaining = k
    
    # Go through upper triangle (including diagonal) in row-major order
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i, n):
            if remaining == 0:
                break
            if i == j:  # diagonal element
                if remaining >= 1:
                    matrix[i][j] = 1
                    remaining -= 1
            else:  # off-diagonal element (i < j)
                if remaining >= 2:
                    matrix[i][j] = 1
                    matrix[j][i] = 1
                    remaining -= 2

    if remaining > 0:
        print(-1)
    else:
        for row in matrix:
            print(' '.join(map(str, row)))
```

But can we achieve any k ≤ n²?

- If k is odd, we can use (k-1)/2 symmetric pairs and one diagonal element
- Maximum symmetric pairs is n*(n-1)//2, maximum diagonal is n
- Total maximum ones = n + 2*(n*(n-1)//2) = n + n*(n-1) = n²

For any k ≤ n², we can achieve it by using diagonal elements (1 each) and symmetric pairs (2 each).

So the only impossibility is when k > n².

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×n matrix filled with zeros initially
2. We need to place exactly k ones in the matrix
3. The matrix must be symmetric with respect to the main diagonal (meaning matrix[i][j] = matrix[j][i])
4. The matrix should be lexicographically maximal
5. If impossible, return -1

## Key Insights

1. **Symmetry constraint**: For a symmetric matrix, if we place a 1 at position (i,j) where i ≠ j, we must also place a 1 at position (j,i). This means each off-diagonal pair consumes 2 ones.

2. **Diagonal elements**: For positions where i = j (main diagonal), placing a 1 only consumes 1 one.

3. **Lexicographic maximality**: To make the matrix lexicographically maximal, we should prioritize placing ones in earlier rows and earlier columns.

4. **Feasibility**: 
   - For off-diagonal pairs: each pair (i,j) where i < j requires 2 ones
   - For diagonal elements: each diagonal position requires 1 one
   - Total ones needed = (number of off-diagonal pairs) × 2 + (number of diagonal ones)

## Strategy

1. First, check if placing k ones is possible:
   - We can place at most n ones on the diagonal (one per diagonal position)
   - For off-diagonal, we can place at most n*(n-1)/2 pairs, each requiring 2 ones
   - Maximum possible ones = n + 2*(n*(n-1)/2) = n + n*(n-1) = n²

2. Since we want lexicographically maximal, we should:
   - Try to fill from top-left to bottom-right
   - For each position (i,j) where i ≤ j (to avoid duplicates due to symmetry):
     - If i = j (diagonal): we can place at most 1 one here
     - If i < j (off-diagonal): placing a one here requires placing another one at (j,i)

3. Algorithm:
   - Start with all zeros
   - For each row i from 0 to n-1:
     - For each column j from i to n-1:
       - If j > i (off-diagonal), we check if we have enough ones remaining to place both (i,j) and (j,i)
       - If j = i (diagonal), we check if we have enough ones remaining
   - Since we want lexicographic maximality, we place ones as early as possible

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Check if k > n² (impossible)
2. Try to place ones in a greedy way from top-left
3. For positions (i,j) where i ≤ j:
   - If i = j: place one only if we have remaining ones and it's beneficial
   - If i < j: place one if we have at least 2 remaining ones (to place both (i,j) and (j,i))

Actually, let me reconsider the lexicographic maximality. Since we want lexicographically maximal, we should fill the matrix row by row, and within each row, column by column.

The optimal strategy:
- Fill all diagonal positions first (each uses 1 one)
- Then fill the off-diagonal positions systematically

But since we want lexicographic maximum, we should prioritize earlier positions.

## Correct Strategy

We should place ones greedily from top-left to bottom-right:

1. For each position (i,j) where i ≤ j:
   - If i = j: we can place at most 1 one here
   - If i < j: we need 2 ones to place at both (i,j) and (j,i)

2. To maximize lexicographically, we should place ones as early as possible.

3. Algorithm:
   - Start with k ones to place
   - For i from 0 to n-1:
     - For j from i to n-1:
       - If j > i and k >= 2: place ones at (i,j) and (j,i), reduce k by 2
       - If j == i and k >= 1: place one at (i,i), reduce k by 1

This ensures lexicographic maximality because we're filling from top-left.

## Edge Cases

- If k > n² (maximum possible ones), return -1
- If k = 0, return all zeros

Let me verify with examples:

Example 1: n=2, k=1
- Max possible ones: 4
- For (0,0): i=j, k>=1, so place one at (0,0), k becomes 0
- For (0,1): i<j, need k>=2, k=0, skip
- For (1,1): i=j, need k>=1, k=0, skip
- Result: [[1,0],[0,0]] ✓

Example 2: n=3, k=2
- (0,0): place one, k=1
- (0,1): need k>=2, k=1, skip  
- (0,2): need k>=2, k=1, skip
- (1,1): need k>=1, k=1, place one, k=0
- (1,2): need k>=2, k=0, skip
- (2,2): need k>=1, k=0, skip
- Result: [[1,0,0],[0,1,0],[0,0,0]] ✓

Example 3: n=2, k=5
- Max possible ones: 4, k=5 > 4, return -1 ✓

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())

# Check if it's possible
max_ones = n * n
if k > max_ones:
    print(-1)
else:
    # Initialize matrix with zeros
    matrix = [[0] * n for _ in range(n)]
    
    # Place ones in lexicographically maximal way
    # Go row by row, column by column (top-left to bottom-right)
    remaining = k
    
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i, n):
            if i == j:  # Diagonal element
                if remaining >= 1:
                    matrix[i][j] = 1
                    remaining -= 1
            else:  # Off-diagonal element (i < j)
                if remaining >= 2:
                    matrix[i][j] = 1
                    matrix[j][i] = 1
                    remaining -= 2
    
    # If we still have remaining ones, it means we have an error (shouldn't happen with valid k)
    # But theoretically, if k > max_ones we already handled this
    if remaining > 0:
        print(-1)
    else:
        for row in matrix:
            print(' '.join(map(str, row)))
```

2. We need to place exactly k ones such that:
   - The matrix is symmetric with respect to the main diagonal (if matrix[i][j] = 1, then matrix[j][i] = 1)
   - The matrix is lexicographically maximal (row by row comparison, first different element determines)
3. If impossible, output -1

## Key Observations

1. **Symmetry constraint**: If we place a 1 at position (i,j) where i ≠ j, we must also place a 1 at (j,i). This means each off-diagonal pair (i,j) and (j,i) requires 2 ones.

2. **Diagonal positions**: If we place a 1 at position (i,i) (main diagonal), it only requires 1 one.

3. **Lexicographic maximality**: To make the matrix lexicographically maximal, we should try to place 1s as early as possible in the row-major order (top rows, then leftmost columns).

4. **Feasibility check**: 
   - Each off-diagonal pair consumes 2 ones
   - Each diagonal position consumes 1 one
   - Total ones needed = number of diagonal ones + 2 × number of off-diagonal pairs
   - Since k can be any value, we need to check if k is achievable

## Strategy

1. **Check feasibility**: 
   - We can place at most n ones on the diagonal (each diagonal position can have at most one 1)
   - For off-diagonal pairs, we can place at most (n*(n-1)/2) pairs, each requiring 2 ones
   - So maximum possible ones = n + 2*(n*(n-1)/2) = n + n*(n-1) = n²
   - But we also need to ensure k is achievable with the constraint that we can only use even numbers for off-diagonal pairs and odd numbers when complemented by diagonal ones

2. **Actually, given the symmetry constraint**:
   - We can place any number of ones on the diagonal (0 to n)
   - For off-diagonal pairs, we can place any number of complete pairs (0 to n*(n-1)/2), each contributing 2 ones
   - So k must be achievable as: k = diagonal_ones + 2 * off_diagonal_pairs
   - This means k ≥ 0, and if k > n² (total possible positions), then impossible
   - But more precisely, k must be such that we can represent it as diagonal_ones + 2 * off_diagonal_pairs where 0 ≤ diagonal_ones ≤ n and 0 ≤ off_diagonal_pairs ≤ n*(n-1)/2

3. **Lexicographic maximality**:
   - We should prioritize placing 1s in the top-left corner first
   - For each row from 0 to n-1, we should try to place 1s from left to right
   - Since we want lexicographic maximum, we should place 1s as early as possible

## Algorithm

1. First check if k is possible:
   - Maximum possible ones is n²
   - Minimum required to place ones is 0
   - But we need k = d + 2*p where d ≤ n, p ≤ n*(n-1)/2
   - If k > n², return -1
   - If k is impossible to represent as d + 2p with constraints, return -1

2. To maximize lexicographically:
   - For each position (i,j) where i ≤ j (to avoid duplicates due to symmetry):
     - If we can place a 1 at (i,j) without exceeding k, do it
     - But we need to ensure symmetry

3. More systematically:
   - We'll try to place 1s from top-left to bottom-right
   - For each position (i,j) where i ≤ j:
     - If we can place a 1 at (i,j), we need to check if it's possible
     - If i = j (diagonal): placing 1 here costs 1
     - If i < j (off-diagonal): placing 1 at (i,j) costs 2 (since (j,i) also gets a 1)

4. To maximize lexicographically, we should greedily place 1s from top-left to bottom-right.

## Step-by-step Solution

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())

# Check if it's possible
max_possible = n * n
if k > max_possible:
    print(-1)
else:
    # We need to represent k as diagonal_ones + 2 * off_diagonal_pairs
    # where 0 <= diagonal_ones <= n and 0 <= off_diagonal_pairs <= n*(n-1)//2
    
    # First, check if k can be represented in this form
    # diagonal_ones can be from 0 to n
    # off_diagonal_pairs can be from 0 to n*(n-1)//2
    
    # The maximum off-diagonal pairs we can have is n*(n-1)//2
    max_off_diagonal_pairs = n * (n - 1) // 2
    
    # If k is too large for the off-diagonal pairs, we need diagonal ones
    # We can have at most n diagonal ones
    # So k must be <= n + 2 * max_off_diagonal_pairs = n + n*(n-1) = n*n
    
    # The constraint is: k should not exceed n*n, which we already checked
    
    # But we also need: k >= 0, and k - d should be non-negative and even
    # and k - d <= 2 * max_off_diagonal_pairs
    
    # So we need: 0 <= k - d <= 2 * max_off_diagonal_pairs and 0 <= d <= n
    # This means d must be in range [max(0, k - 2*max_off_diagonal_pairs), min(n, k)]
    
    # Since k <= n*n and n*n = n + n*(n-1), we have k - 2*max_off_diagonal_pairs = k - n*(n-1)
    # and -k + n*(n-1) <= d <= min(n, k) (with d >= 0)
    
    # For feasibility: we need k - 2*max_off_diagonal_pairs <= n
    # k - n*(n-1) <= n
    # k <= n*(n-1) + n = n*n, which is true
    
    # So as long as k <= n*n, there exists a valid d
    
    # Actually, the only constraint is that k must be representable as d + 2p
    # where p is an integer and 0 <= p <= max_off_diagonal_pairs
    
    # This is possible iff k - d is even and 0 <= k - d <= 2*max_off_diagonal_pairs
    # and 0 <= d <= n
    
    # For any k <= n*n, we can always find such d?
    
    # Let's check: we need to find d such that:
    # 1. 0 <= d <= n
    # 2. k - d is even and non-negative
    # 3. k - d <= 2 * max_off_diagonal_pairs
    
    # Since k - d <= 2*max_off_diagonal_pairs means d >= k - 2*max_off_diagonal_pairs
    # And d >= 0, so d >= max(0, k - 2*max_off_diagonal_pairs)
    
    # We also need k - d to be even, so d and k must have the same parity
    
    # Since we can choose d, we just need to find any d in [max(0, k - 2*max_off_diagonal_pairs), min(n, k)] 
    # such that d ≡ k (mod 2)
    
    # This is always possible because the interval length is at least 1 (since k <= n*n and n*n >= n for n >= 1)
    # and in any consecutive integers, there are at least one pair with the same parity
    # Actually, if the interval has length >= 1, we can always find such d
    
    # But what if k > n*n? We already checked that
    
    # So the only impossibility is when k > n*n
    
    # Therefore, if k > n*n, output -1, otherwise we can always construct it
    
    if k > n * n:
        print(-1)
    else:
        # Now construct the lexicographically maximal matrix
        # We'll use a greedy approach: try to place 1s from top-left to bottom-right
        # But due to symmetry, we only place for i <= j
        
        matrix = [[0] * n for _ in range(n)]
        
        # First, we need to decide how many diagonal ones and off-diagonal pairs
        # We want lexicographic maximum, so we should place 1s as early as possible
        
        # For lexicographic maximality, we should prioritize earlier positions
        # We can work row by row, left to right
        
        remaining = k
        off_diagonal_count = 0
        
        # Process positions where i <= j (to avoid duplicates)
        for i in range(n):
            for j in range(i, n):
                if remaining == 0:
                    break
                    
                # If we place a 1 at (i,j)
                if i == j:
                    # Cost is 1
                    if remaining >= 1:
                        matrix[i][j] = 1
                        remaining -= 1
                else:
                    # Cost is 2 (both (i,j) and (j,i))
                    if remaining >= 2:
                        matrix[i][j] = 1
                        matrix[j][i] = 1
                        remaining -= 2
        
        # If we still have remaining ones to place, it's impossible
        if remaining > 0:
            print(-1)
        else:
            for row in matrix:
                print(' '.join(map(str, row)))
```

- We can place 1 diagonal: (0,0) costs 1
- We can place off-diagonal: (0,1) and (1,0) costs 2
- So k=1 is possible by placing at (0,0)

- We can place at (0,0) costs 1, then (0,1) and (1,0) costs 2, total 3

- Maximum is 4
- Can we have 3 ones?
- We can have 1 diagonal (cost 1) and 1 off-diagonal pair (cost 2), total cost 3
- So yes

Check if any k ≤ n*n is impossible?
- k must be representable as diagonal_ones + 2*off_diagonal_pairs
- With diagonal_ones from 0 to n, and off_diagonal_pairs from 0 to n*(n-1)//2
- The set of achievable values is all integers from 0 to n*n, because we can increment by 1 by choosing different combinations

Actually, we can only increment by 1 on diagonal, and by 2 on off-diagonal pairs.

But if we can always find a diagonal position to use, then we can achieve any value as long as k ≤ n*n.

We can achieve any integer value from 0 to n*n because:
- We can achieve all even values from 0 to 2*floor(n*(n-1)/2) by using off-diagonal pairs
- We can achieve all odd values from 1 to 2*floor(n*(n-1)/2) + n by using one diagonal and off-diagonal pairs
- Since floor(n*(n-1)/2) = maximum off-diagonal pairs

For example, if n=2: max off-diagonal pairs = 1, so max off-diagonal cost = 2
- Even values: 0, 2 (using 0 or 1 off-diagonal pair)
- Odd values: 1, 3 (using 1 diagonal + 0 off-diagonal, or 1 diagonal + 1 off-diagonal)
- So we can achieve 0,1,2,3

For any n, we can achieve:
- All values that are at most n*n
- Since we can use any number of diagonal ones (0 to n) and any number of off-diagonal pairs (0 to n*(n-1)/2), and we can change the total by 1 each time (by using diagonal rather than off-diagonal), we should be able to achieve any value from 0 to n*n.

Actually, the range is from 0 to n*n, and it's connected with steps of 1 (since we can increase by 1 by adding a diagonal one), so yes, any k from 0 to n*n is achievable.

Therefore, the only impossibility is when k > n*n.

So algorithm:

1. If k > n*n, output -1
2. Otherwise, construct the matrix greedily:
   - For each position (i,j) where i <= j, in row-major order:
     - If i == j (diagonal): if remaining >= 1, place 1 at (i,j) and reduce remaining by 1
     - If i < j (off-diagonal): if remaining >= 2, place 1 at (i,j) and (j,i) and reduce remaining by 2
   - This ensures lexicographically maximal because we go row by row, left to right

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())

# Maximum possible ones in n*n matrix
max_possible = n * n

if k > max_possible:
    print(-1)
else:
    matrix = [[0] * n for _ in range(n)]
    remaining = k
    
    # Fill positions in lexicographic order (row-major, i <= j)
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i, n):
            if remaining == 0:
                break
                
            if i == j:
                # Diagonal position: costs 1
                if remaining >= 1:
                    matrix[i][j] = 1
                    remaining -= 1
            else:
                # Off-diagonal position: costs 2 due to symmetry
                if remaining >= 2:
                    matrix[i][j] = 1
                    matrix[j][i] = 1
                    remaining -= 2
    
    # If we still have remaining ones, it should not happen given our analysis
    if remaining > 0:
        print(-1)
    else:
        for row in matrix:
            print(' '.join(map(str, row)))
```

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

You are given matrix with n rows and n columns filled with zeroes. You should put k ones in it in such a way that the resulting matrix is symmetrical with respect to the main diagonal (the diagonal that goes from the top left to the bottom right corner) and is lexicographically maximal.

One matrix is lexicographically greater than the other if the first different number in the first different row from the top in the first matrix is greater than the corresponding number in the second one.

If there exists no such matrix then output -1.

-----Output-----

Output

Output

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())

# Check if k exceeds the maximum possible ones in a symmetric matrix
max_ones = n * n
if k > max_ones:
    print(-1)
else:
    # Create n x n matrix filled with zeros
    matrix = [[0] * n for _ in range(n)]
    
    # Place diagonal ones first (each uses 1 one)
    for i in range(n):
        if k >= 1:
            matrix[i][i] = 1
            k -= 1
    
    # Place off-diagonal pairs (each pair uses 2 ones)
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i + 1, n):
            if k >= 2:
                matrix[i][j] = 1
                matrix[j][i] = 1
                k -= 2
    
    # If we still have ones left, it means our calculation is wrong
    # But due to our constraints, k should be exactly used up
    if k > 0:
        print(-1)
    else:
        for row in matrix:
            print(' '.join(map(str, row)))
```

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an n×n matrix initially filled with zeros
2. We need to place exactly k ones in it
3. The matrix must be symmetric with respect to the main diagonal (if cell (i,j) has a 1, then cell (j,i) must also have a 1)
4. The matrix should be lexicographically maximal (first different element in the first different row should be greater)
5. If no such matrix exists, output -1

## Key Insights

1. **Symmetry constraint**: For every (i,j) where we place a 1, we must also place a 1 at (j,i). This means:
   - If i ≠ j: placing a 1 at (i,j) requires another 1 at (j,i) = 2 ones used
   - If i = j: placing a 1 at (i,i) uses only 1 one

2. **Lexicographically maximal**: To make the matrix lexicographically maximal, we should place ones as early as possible in the row ordering (top to bottom), and within each row, as early as possible in the column ordering (left to right).

3. **Feasibility check**: 
   - For off-diagonal elements: each pair (i,j) where i≠j consumes 2 ones
   - For diagonal elements: each (i,i) consumes 1 one
   - The total number of ones k must be achievable with these constraints

4. **Strategy**:
   - We need to decide how many ones go on the diagonal and how many go off-diagonal
   - We want to maximize lexicographical order, so we should prioritize placing ones in the top-left corner
   - For off-diagonal placement: we should place them in positions that increase lexicographically (like (0,1), (0,2), etc.)
   - For diagonal placement: we should place them on the main diagonal

## Step-by-step Solution

1. **Check if solution is possible**:
   - Let `d` be the number of diagonal ones (0 ≤ d ≤ n)
   - Let `o` be the number of off-diagonal pairs (each pair contributes 2 ones)
   - We need: d + 2×o = k
   - So k must be at least 0, and if k > n, we can only place at most n diagonal ones
   - The maximum possible ones is n (all diagonal) + 2×(number of possible off-diagonal pairs)

2. **For the lexicographically maximal arrangement**:
   - We want to place the ones as early as possible in the matrix
   - So we should fill the diagonal first, then fill off-diagonal positions in the order (0,1), (0,2), ..., (0,n-1), (1,2), ..., (n-2,n-1)

3. **Feasibility conditions**:
   - If k > n + 2×(n*(n-1)//2), then it's impossible
   - The maximum number of ones possible is n (diagonal) + 2×(number of off-diagonal pairs)
   - The number of off-diagonal pairs = n*(n-1)//2
   - So maximum ones = n + 2×(n*(n-1)//2) = n + n*(n-1) = n²

4. **Actually, maximum possible k is n²**, but due to symmetry, when placing off-diagonal pairs, each pair uses 2 ones.

5. **Construction**:
   - First, try to place as many diagonal ones as possible from the top-left
   - Then place off-diagonal ones in lexicographic order

- For diagonal (i,i): if remaining_ones >= 1, place a 1 at (i,i), reduce by 1
- For off-diagonal with i < j: if remaining_ones >= 2, place 1s at (i,j) and (j,i), reduce by 2

This ensures lexicographical maximality because we're placing ones as early as possible.

Perfect!

```python
n, k = map(int, input().split())

# Maximum possible ones in a symmetric n x n matrix
max_ones = n * n

if k > max_ones:
    print(-1)
else:
    # Initialize matrix with zeros
    matrix = [[0] * n for _ in range(n)]
    remaining = k
    
    # Fill matrix in row-major order, prioritizing diagonal then upper triangle
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(n):
            if i == j:  # Diagonal element
                if remaining >= 1:
                    matrix[i][j] = 1
                    remaining -= 1
            elif i < j:  # Upper triangle (i < j), symmetric to (j,i)
                if remaining >= 2:
                    matrix[i][j] = 1
                    matrix[j][i] = 1
                    remaining -= 2
            # For i > j, we don't need to do anything because of symmetry
    
    # Output the matrix
    for row in matrix:
        print(' '.join(map(str, row)))
```

else:

    matrix = [[0] * n for _ in range(n)]
    remaining = k

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

In the probability theory the following paradox called Benford's law is known: "In many lists of random numbers taken from real sources, numbers starting with digit 1 occur much more often than numbers starting with any other digit" (that's the simplest form of the law).

Having read about it on Codeforces, the Hedgehog got intrigued by the statement and wishes to thoroughly explore it. He finds the following similar problem interesting in particular: there are N random variables, the i-th of which can take any integer value from some segment [Li;Ri] (all numbers from this segment are equiprobable). It means that the value of the i-th quantity can be equal to any integer number from a given interval [Li;Ri] with probability 1 / (Ri - Li + 1).

The Hedgehog wants to know the probability of the event that the first digits of at least K% of those values will be equal to one. In other words, let us consider some set of fixed values of these random variables and leave only the first digit (the MSD — most significant digit) of each value. Then let's count how many times the digit 1 is encountered and if it is encountered in at least K per cent of those N values, than such set of values will be called a good one. You have to find the probability that a set of values of the given random variables will be a good one.

Input

The first line contains number N which is the number of random variables (1 ≤ N ≤ 1000). Then follow N lines containing pairs of numbers Li, Ri, each of whom is a description of a random variable. It is guaranteed that 1 ≤ Li ≤ Ri ≤ 1018.

The last line contains an integer K (0 ≤ K ≤ 100).

All the numbers in the input file are integers.

Please, do not use %lld specificator to read or write 64-bit integers in C++. It is preffered to use cin (also you may use %I64d).

Output

Print the required probability. Print the fractional number with such a precision that the relative or absolute error of the result won't exceed 10 - 9.

Examples

Input

1
1 2
50


Output

0.500000000000000

Input

2
1 2
9 11
50


Output

0.833333333333333

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

Now solve the problem by providing the code.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.
```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

Model Response

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have N random variables, each taking values from [Li, Ri] with uniform probability
2. We need to find the probability that at least K% of these values have 1 as their first digit (most significant digit)
3. For each random variable, we need to calculate the probability that its value starts with digit 1

## Key Insights

1. For a number to start with digit 1, it must be in the range [1, 1.999...], [10, 19.999...], [100, 199.999...], etc.
2. In general, a number x has first digit 1 if x ∈ [10^k, 2×10^k) for some k ≥ 0
3. We need to find the probability that at least K% of N variables have first digit 1

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Calculate probability for each variable to start with digit 1

For each interval [Li, Ri], we need to find how many numbers in this range start with digit 1.

For a given range [L, R], the count of numbers starting with digit 1 is the sum over all powers of 10:
- Count numbers in [10^k, min(2×10^k, R+1)) that are ≥ L
- This is equivalent to counting numbers in [max(L, 10^k), min(2×10^k, R+1))

### Step 2: Apply probability principle

For each variable i, calculate P_i = (count of numbers starting with 1 in [Li, Ri]) / (Ri - Li + 1)

### Step 3: Find probability that at least K% of variables have first digit 1

This is a binomial-like probability where each variable independently has probability P_i of having first digit 1.

We need P(at least (K/100)*N variables have first digit 1).

Since K% of N values means at least K*N/100 variables, we can:
- For each possible count j from ceil(K*N/100) to N
- Calculate the probability that exactly j variables have first digit 1
- Sum these probabilities

### Step 4: Implementation details

We need to efficiently calculate the count of numbers in [L, R] that start with digit 1.

For a range [L, R], we can:
1. For each power of 10 (1, 10, 100, 1000, ...):
   - Find the intersection of [L, R] with [10^k, 2*10^k)
   - Add the count of numbers in this intersection
2. Be careful with boundaries (inclusive/exclusive)

```python
def count_start_with_one(L, R):
    """Count numbers in [L, R] that start with digit 1"""
    if L > R:
        return 0
    
    count = 0
    power = 1
    
    while power <= R:
        # Range [10^k, 2*10^k) 
        low_bound = power
        high_bound = min(2 * power, R + 1)
        
        # Find intersection with [L, R]
        start = max(L, low_bound)
        end = min(R, high_bound)
        
        if start <= end:
            count += end - start + 1
            
        power *= 10
    
    return count

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    variables = []
    for _ in range(n):
        l, r = map(int, input().split())
        variables.append((l, r))
    
    k_percent = int(input())
    min_needed = (k_percent * n + 50) // 100  # ceil(k_percent * n / 100)
    
    # Calculate probability for each variable to start with digit 1
    probs = []
    for l, r in variables:
        count = count_start_with_one(l, r)
        total = r - l + 1
        prob = count / total
        probs.append(prob)
    
    # Calculate probability that at least min_needed variables have first digit 1
    # This is sum over j = min_needed to n of (sum of combinations)
    # We can use dynamic programming or direct sum
    
    # Use dynamic programming: dp[i][j] = probability that first i variables have j variables with first digit 1
    dp = [0.0] * (n + 1)
    dp[0] = 1.0
    
    for prob in probs:
        new_dp = [0.0] * (n + 1)
        for j in range(n + 1):
            if dp[j] > 0:
                # If we don't pick this variable
                new_dp[j] += dp[j] * (1 - prob)
                # If we pick this variable
                if j + 1 <= n:
                    new_dp[j + 1] += dp[j] * prob
        dp = new_dp
    
    result = sum(dp[min_needed:])
    print(f"{result:.15f}")

solve()
```

Wait, I need to be more careful with the geometric series calculation. Let me verify the algorithm:

The function `count_start_with_one` correctly identifies numbers that start with digit 1 by checking ranges [10^k, 2*10^k) for each k. The boundaries are handled properly.

For the dynamic programming, I'm building the probability distribution step by step, which is correct.

Let me trace through the examples:

Example 1: N=1, [1,2], K=50
- Numbers: 1, 2
- 1 starts with 1, 2 starts with 2
- Count = 1, total = 2, prob = 0.5
- Need at least 50% of 1 = 0.5, so need at least 1 variable
- Probability = 0.5 ✓

Example 2: N=2, [1,2], [9,11], K=50
- First variable: prob = 0.5 (as above)
- Second variable: numbers 9,10,11. 
  - 9: first digit 9, 10: first digit 1, 11: first digit 1
  - Count = 2, total = 3, prob = 2/3
- Need at least 50% of 2 = 1 variable
- Probability = P(exactly 1) + P(exactly 2)
- P(exactly 1) = 0.5*(1-2/3) + (1-0.5)*(2/3) = 0.5*(1/3) + 0.5*(2/3) = 1/6 + 2/6 = 3/6 = 0.5
- P(exactly 2) = 0.5*(2/3) = 1/3
- Total = 0.5 + 1/3 = 5/6 ≈ 0.833... ✓

```python
def count_start_with_one(L, R):
    """Count numbers in [L, R] that start with digit 1"""
    if L > R:
        return 0
    
    count = 0
    power = 1
    
    while power <= R:
        # Range [10^k, 2*10^k) 
        low_bound = power
        high_bound = min(2 * power, R + 1)
        
        # Find intersection with [L, R]
        start = max(L, low_bound)
        end = min(R, high_bound)
        
        if start <= end:
            count += end - start + 1
            
        power *= 10
    
    return count
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

In the probability theory the following paradox called Benford's law is known: "In many lists of random numbers taken from real sources, numbers starting with digit 1 occur much more often than numbers starting with any other digit" (that's the simplest form of the law).

Having read about it on Codeforces, the Hedgehog got intrigued by the statement and wishes to thoroughly explore it. He finds the following similar problem interesting in particular: there are N random variables, the i-th of which can take any integer value from some segment [Li;Ri] (all numbers from this segment are equiprobable). It means that the value of the i-th quantity can be equal to any integer number from a given interval [Li;Ri] with probability 1 / (Ri - Li + 1).

The Hedgehog wants to know the probability of the event that the first digits of at least K% of those values will be equal to one. In other words, let us consider some set of fixed values of these random variables and leave only the first digit (the MSD — most significant digit) of each value. Then let's count how many times the digit 1 is encountered and if it is encountered in at least K per cent of those N values, than such set of values will be called a good one. You have to find the probability that a set of values of the given random variables will be a good one.

Input

The first line contains number N which is the number of random variables (1 ≤ N ≤ 1000). Then follow N lines containing pairs of numbers Li, Ri, each of whom is a description of a random variable. It is guaranteed that 1 ≤ Li ≤ Ri ≤ 1018.

The last line contains an integer K (0 ≤ K ≤ 100).

All the numbers in the input file are integers.

Please, do not use %lld specificator to read or write 64-bit integers in C++. It is preffered to use cin (also you may use %I64d).

Output

Print the required probability. Print the fractional number with such a precision that the relative or absolute error of the result won't exceed 10 - 9.

Examples

Input

1
1 2
50


Output

0.500000000000000

Input

2
1 2
9 11
50


Output

0.833333333333333

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

Now solve the problem by providing the code.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.
```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have N random variables, each taking integer values from [Li, Ri] with equal probability
2. For each variable, we look at its most significant digit (MSD)
3. We want the probability that at least K% of the values have MSD = 1
4. This means we need at least (K/100) * N values to have MSD = 1

## Key Insights

1. For any number, we can find its MSD by repeatedly dividing by 10 until we get a single digit
2. For a number to have MSD = 1, it must be in the range [1, 1.999...], [10, 19.999...], [100, 199.999...], etc.
3. For a given interval [Li, Ri], we need to count how many numbers in that interval have MSD = 1

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Function to count numbers with MSD = 1 in range [L, R]

For a number to have MSD = 1, it must be in one of these ranges:
- [1, 1]
- [10, 19]
- [100, 199]
- [1000, 1999]
- etc.

So for a range [L, R], we need to count how many numbers in [L, R] fall into any of these "1-first-digit" ranges.

### Step 2: For each variable, calculate probability of having MSD = 1

For each interval [Li, Ri], count how many numbers have MSD = 1, then divide by (Ri - Li + 1).

### Step 3: Use dynamic programming or combinatorics to find the probability that at least K% have MSD = 1

Actually, since N ≤ 1000, we can use dynamic programming where:
- dp[i][j] = probability that after considering first i variables, exactly j of them have MSD = 1
- We need to find the probability that j ≥ (K/100) * N

### Step 4: Implementation details

1. For each variable, compute p[i] = probability that it has MSD = 1
2. Use DP to compute the probability distribution over the number of variables with MSD = 1
3. Sum the probabilities where count ≥ (K/100) * N

```python
def msd_is_one(n):
    """Check if the most significant digit of n is 1"""
    if n == 0:
        return False
    while n >= 10:
        n //= 10
    return n == 1

def count_msd_one_in_range(l, r):
    """Count numbers in [l, r] that have MSD = 1"""
    count = 0
    # Check each power of 10
    power = 1
    while power <= r:
        # Range [power * 1, power * 10 - 1] has MSD = 1
        low = power
        high = min(power * 10 - 1, r)
        
        if low <= high:
            # Find intersection with [l, r]
            start = max(l, low)
            end = min(r, high)
            if start <= end:
                count += end - start + 1
        
        # Move to next power of 10
        power *= 10
    
    return count

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    intervals = []
    for _ in range(n):
        l, r = map(int, input().split())
        intervals.append((l, r))
    
    k = int(input())
    
    # Calculate probability for each variable to have MSD = 1
    p = []
    for l, r in intervals:
        count = count_msd_one_in_range(l, r)
        total = r - l + 1
        prob = count / total if total > 0 else 0
        p.append(prob)
    
    # Calculate probability that at least (k/100) * n variables have MSD = 1
    threshold = (k / 100.0) * n
    
    # If threshold is 0, at least 0% means at least 0 variables have MSD = 1
    # which is always true, so probability is 1 if k=0
    if k == 0:
        print("1.000000000000000")
        return
    
    # Use dynamic programming
    # dp[i][j] = probability that exactly j variables have MSD = 1 after considering first i variables
    # We only need the previous state, so use 1D array
    
    # Maximum possible count is n
    dp = [0.0] * (n + 1)
    dp[0] = 1.0  # Before any variables, 0 variables have MSD = 1 with probability 1
    
    for prob in p:
        new_dp = [0.0] * (n + 1)
        for j in range(n + 1):
            if dp[j] > 0:
                # Current state: j variables have MSD = 1
                # With probability (1 - prob), this stays the same
                new_dp[j] += dp[j] * (1 - prob)
                # With probability prob, we add one more variable with MSD = 1
                if j + 1 <= n:
                    new_dp[j + 1] += dp[j] * prob
        dp = new_dp
    
    # Sum probabilities where count >= threshold
    result = 0.0
    for j in range(int(threshold), n + 1):
        result += dp[j]
    
    print(f"{result:.15f}")

solve()
```

Wait, I need to be more careful with the threshold. Since we need at least K%, and K might not divide evenly, I should consider that we need at least ceil(K/100 * N) variables, but actually it's "at least K%", so it's any value >= K/100 * N.

Let me fix the implementation:

```python
def count_msd_one_in_range(l, r):
    """Count numbers in [l, r] that have MSD = 1"""
    count = 0
    # Check each power of 10
    power = 1
    while power <= r:
        # Range [power * 1, power * 10 - 1] has MSD = 1
        low = power
        high = min(power * 10 - 1, r)
        
        if low <= high:
            # Find intersection with [l, r]
            start = max(l, low)
            end = min(r, high)
            if start <= end:
                count += end - start + 1
        
        # Move to next power of 10
        power *= 10
    
    return count

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    intervals = []
    for _ in range(n):
        l, r = map(int, input().split())
        intervals.append((l, r))
    
    k = int(input())
    
    # Calculate probability for each variable to have MSD = 1
    p = []
    for l, r in intervals:
        count = count_msd_one_in_range(l, r)
        total = r - l + 1
        prob = count / total if total > 0 else 0
        p.append(prob)
    
    # If k is 0, then we need at least 0% of variables to have MSD = 1
    # Since 0% is always true, the probability is 1
    if k == 0:
        print("1.000000000000000")
        return
    
    # Calculate minimum number of variables needed (at least k%)
    threshold = (k / 100.0) * n
    
    # Use dynamic programming to find probability distribution
    # dp[j] = probability that exactly j variables have MSD = 1
    dp = [0.0] * (n + 1)
    dp[0] = 1.0
    
    for prob in p:
        new_dp = [0.0] * (n + 1)
        for j in range(n + 1):
            if dp[j] > 0:
                # Don't get MSD = 1
                new_dp[j] += dp[j] * (1 - prob)
                # Get MSD = 1
                if j + 1 <= n:
                    new_dp[j + 1] += dp[j] * prob
        dp = new_dp
    
    # Sum probabilities for all counts >= threshold
    result = 0.0
    for j in range(int(threshold), n + 1):
        result += dp[j]
    
    print(f"{result:.15f}")

solve()
```

        # Range [power * 1, power * 10 - 1] has MSD = 1

            end = min(r, high)
            if start <= end:
                count += end - start + 1
        
        # Move to next power of 10
        power *= 10
    
    return count
